<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Writing strong hypotheses

```text
Let's test a new product page. Sound familiar? That's not a hypothesis. It's a wish. And tests built on wishes usually end in a shrug: inconclusive, nobody learned anything. In this lecture you'll learn why hypotheses matter, a template that forces clarity, the qualities of a strong one, how to turn one insight into several hypotheses, and how to choose primary, secondary and guardrail metrics. Then you'll watch me use AI to widen my idea pool without letting it invent evidence.
```

Scene 2 — Why hypotheses matter

```text
Why do hypotheses matter? Three reasons. They connect a test to evidence, so you're testing something research suggests could work. They define success in advance, so nobody can move the goalposts after the results come in. And they make every result useful, including losses. If a test built on a clear mechanism loses, you learn the mechanism was wrong, or the execution was weak. If a test built on a wish loses, you learn nothing. Hypotheses turn tests into learning.
```

Scene 3 — The template

```text
Here's the template. Because we observed this insight, from this evidence, we believe that making this change, for this audience, will cause this outcome, measured by this metric. Every part matters. Because forces evidence. Change forces specificity. Audience forces focus. Outcome and metric force a definition of success. Think of it like a doctor's prescription. It names the diagnosis, the treatment, the patient, and how you'll know it worked.
```

Scene 4 — Qualities of a strong hypothesis

```text
What makes a hypothesis strong? It's grounded in evidence from more than one source where possible. It's specific enough that a designer could build it without guessing. It names a mechanism, why it should work, like reducing anxiety or removing friction. It's testable with your traffic. And it's worth doing: if it wins, the impact would matter to the business. Tagging the mechanism is especially useful, because over time your learning library tells you which mechanisms work for your customers.
```

Scene 5 — One insight, several hypotheses

```text
One insight can produce several hypotheses. Say research shows mobile visitors don't trust that delivery will arrive on time. You could add a delivery date estimate on the product page. You could show a delivery promise in the header. You could add reviews that mention delivery. You could offer a delivery guarantee with compensation if you can honour it. Each addresses the same cause with a different change. Write several, then prioritise. The first idea is rarely the best one.
```

Scene 6 — Three kinds of metric

```text
Now metrics. The primary metric decides the test: choose the one closest to business value that you can measure within the test, often revenue per visitor or purchase conversion. Secondary metrics help you understand how it worked, like add-to-cart rate. Guardrail metrics protect you from harmful wins: return rate, refund requests, page speed, support contacts. And decide your minimum worthwhile effect, the smallest improvement that would justify shipping. That becomes the input for your sample size calculation in the next module.
```

Scene 7 — Simple example: agency audit form

```text
A simple example. An agency sells a free SEO audit through a landing page. The weak hypothesis: let's make the form shorter. The strong one: because fourteen of twenty form recordings show people pausing at the website revenue field, and sales says that field rarely changes the audit, removing it for first-time visitors will increase qualified audit requests, measured by sales-accepted leads, with lead quality as a guardrail. Notice the primary metric isn't form submissions. A shorter form could double submissions and halve quality.
```

Scene 8 — Realistic example: UK fashion fit guide (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic scenario with illustrative details. A UK fashion brand found that eleven of twenty-five filtered mobile recordings showed repeated taps on the size selector before exit, and thirty-eight percent of poll answers mentioned fit. The hypothesis: because mobile visitors from paid social can't judge fit, adding a find-my-size guide with model height and a fit summary from reviews above the size selector will increase revenue per visitor. Secondary metric: add-to-cart rate. Guardrails: thirty-day return rate and mobile page speed. Minimum worthwhile effect: five percent relative. That's a hypothesis a designer can build, an analyst can evaluate, and a finance director can understand.
```

Scene 9 — Watch me do it: AI to widen ideas

```text
Watch me use AI to widen my idea pool. I paste the finding, with its evidence counts, into my assistant. I ask for six different changes that could address the underlying cause, from small copy tweaks to bigger UX changes. For each, I want the mechanism, what would have to be true for it to work, and one risk or guardrail. And I add a rule: no dark patterns, no fake urgency, no hidden costs, no pre-ticked boxes. It suggests a fit guide, a review-based fit summary, a size-exchange promise, a try-on video, a fit quiz and a size recommendation from past purchases. I keep the ones that trace back to evidence, and write them up as full hypotheses in the card format.
```

Scene 10 — Store hypotheses as structured cards

```text
Store hypotheses in a structured format, not scattered across slides. A spreadsheet row, a ticket in your project tool, or a small YAML file in a repository. Each gets an ID, links to the findings that support it, the page and audience, the because, change and expect statements, a mechanism tag, the primary, secondary and guardrail metrics, the minimum worthwhile effect, and a status. Structure makes hypotheses searchable, easy to prioritise, and easy to link to results later. It also makes them readable by tools, including AI assistants that help you search your learning library.
```

Scene 11 — Hypotheses for non-test changes

```text
Hypotheses aren't only for A/B tests. Some changes you'll simply build, like fixing a broken payment method, and some you'll drop. Write a short hypothesis for those too. It records why you made the change and what you expect, so you can check afterwards whether the metric moved in the expected direction, even without a controlled test. It keeps your decisions honest and your learning library complete.
```

Scene 12 — Practice: rewrite a weak hypothesis

```text
Let's practise rewriting a weak hypothesis, because this is where most teams improve fastest. Weak: adding trust badges will increase conversions. What's missing? Evidence: why do we think trust is the problem? Specificity: which badges, where? Audience: everyone, or first-time mobile visitors? Mechanism: trust in what, exactly, the payment or the product? And the metric: conversions of what? Strong: because exit poll answers on the payment step mention card safety, and recordings show pauses at the card field, adding the payment provider's security explanation and accepted wallets next to the card field for first-time visitors will increase checkout completion, with payment errors as a guardrail. Same idea, but now it can be built, tested and learned from.
```

Scene 13 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Hypotheses without evidence. Vague changes, like improve the page. Missing audience. Clicks or form fills as the primary metric when the business cares about revenue or qualified leads. No guardrails. Too many primary metrics. And letting AI-generated ideas skip the evidence step. Each of these produces tests that are hard to interpret, or wins that don't matter.
```

Scene 14 — Recap

```text
Recap. A hypothesis connects evidence to a specific change, audience, outcome and metric. Strong hypotheses name a mechanism and define success before the test. One insight can produce many hypotheses, and AI can help you widen the pool, as long as ideas trace back to evidence. Choose a primary metric close to business value, add secondary and guardrail metrics, and set a minimum worthwhile effect. Try this now: take your strongest research finding, write three hypotheses in the card format from the lesson text, and tag each with its mechanism.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Prioritisation with ICE, PIE and evidence scoring

```text
You have forty ideas and capacity for three tests this month. Which three? If the answer is whichever the most senior person likes, this lecture is for you. You'll learn why formal prioritisation matters, how ICE, PIE and evidence-weighted scoring work, how to deal with traffic constraints, and how to run a scoring session that the team trusts. Then you'll watch me score a backlog with a short script that makes the rules visible to everyone.
```

Scene 2 — Why prioritise formally

```text
Why prioritise formally? Because capacity is the scarcest resource in CRO. Development time, design time and above all traffic limit how many good tests you can run. Formal prioritisation makes the trade-offs visible. It reduces the influence of the loudest voice, sometimes called the HiPPO, the highest paid person's opinion. And it creates a record of why you chose what you chose, so you can improve your judgement over time.
```

Scene 3 — ICE

```text
ICE is the simplest framework. Score each idea from one to ten on impact, how much it could move the metric; confidence, how sure you are it will work; and ease, how easy it is to build and test. Multiply or average the three. It's fast and good for small teams. The weakness: impact and confidence are subjective, so scores can reflect enthusiasm more than evidence.
```

Scene 4 — PIE

```text
PIE is similar, but page-focused. Potential: how much room for improvement does this page have? Importance: how valuable is the traffic on this page? Ease: how easy is it to change? It's useful for deciding which pages to work on first. But it shares ICE's weakness. Both frameworks depend heavily on judgement, which is where evidence-weighted scoring comes in.
```

Scene 5 — Evidence-weighted scoring

```text
Evidence-weighted scoring, inspired by frameworks like PXL, replaces vague ratings with yes-or-no questions. Is the change above the fold or on a high-traffic template? Is it linked to a logged research finding? How many independent methods support it? Does it reduce anxiety or friction? Is it close to the conversion point? And how much effort is it? Each yes adds points, effort subtracts. Because the questions are factual, two people scoring the same idea tend to agree. And ideas with evidence rise to the top.
```

Scene 6 — Simple example: Islamabad bakery

```text
A simple example. A small online bakery in Islamabad has three ideas. A new homepage banner: no research link, medium effort. Adding delivery cut-off times to product pages: linked to support chats and a poll, small effort, close to purchase. And a loyalty programme: some survey support, large effort. Evidence-weighted scoring puts delivery cut-off times first, by a clear margin. The banner drops, because nobody could point to evidence it would help. The loyalty programme stays in the backlog for later research.
```

Scene 7 — Which framework when?

```text
Let's compare the three frameworks side by side, so you know when to use each. ICE works well for a small team with a short backlog, when you need to decide quickly and everyone shares context. PIE works well when you're deciding which pages or templates to focus on for the next quarter. Evidence-weighted scoring works best when the backlog is large, several people contribute ideas, and you need decisions that people trust. Many teams combine them: PIE to choose the focus pages, then evidence-weighted scoring for ideas on those pages. Whatever you choose, write the rules down and keep them stable for a few months, so scores are comparable over time.
```

Scene 8 — Traffic constraints

```text
Traffic changes everything. A brilliant idea on a page with little traffic may be untestable. Before an idea reaches the top of your list, check feasibility. How many visitors and conversions does this page get? What's the minimum worthwhile effect? How long would a test take? If the answer is six months, it's not a test candidate. Build it with monitoring, test it on a higher-traffic template, research it more, or drop it. Add a feasibility column to your backlog so this check happens every time.
```

Scene 9 — Realistic example: Dubai agency session (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic scenario with illustrative details. A Dubai agency runs CRO for a regional electronics retailer. The backlog has forty ideas from the research sprint. In a ninety-minute session, three people score them independently with evidence-weighted questions, then discuss only the ideas where their scores differ by more than two points. Feasibility checks remove five ideas that would need months of traffic. The top three: delivery estimates on product pages, wallet payment placement in checkout, and clearer warranty information. The client's CEO had wanted a homepage video. It's in the backlog, scored transparently, with a note on what evidence would move it up.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me do it: scoring script

```text
Watch me score with a script. My backlog lives in a CSV file with one row per idea and columns for each yes-or-no question plus effort. The script holds the weights at the top, where everyone can see and challenge them. Linked research finding is worth two points. Each independent evidence source is worth one. High-traffic template, anxiety or friction reduction, and closeness to conversion are one each. Effort subtracts nothing for small, one for medium, two for large. I run it, and it prints a ranked list. The value isn't the code. It's that the rules are explicit, so arguments move from whose idea is best to whether the weights are right.
```

Scene 11 — AI and prioritisation

```text
Can AI score your backlog? It can pre-fill scores from written hypotheses and notes, which speeds up big backlogs. But impact and confidence are exactly the judgements that are easy to fake. So use AI differently: ask it to flag missing evidence and inconsistencies. For example, this hypothesis claims two evidence sources but links only one finding. Or, these two ideas are near-duplicates. Keep the final scores as a team decision.
```

Scene 12 — Running a scoring session

```text
Make scoring a team ritual. Invite the people who own research, design, development and the business goal. Score independently first, so nobody anchors on the boss. Discuss only the big disagreements. Record the final scores and the reason for any override. Rescore the top of the backlog every two weeks as new research arrives. And publish the list, so everyone can see what's next and why.
```

Scene 13 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Scoring by enthusiasm. Letting one person override without recording why. Ignoring effort or traffic. Never rescoring as evidence changes. Treating the score as the decision, instead of an input to discussion. And letting a framework become bureaucracy. A scoring session should take ninety minutes, not a week.
```

Scene 14 — Balance the portfolio

```text
One more thing: prioritisation isn't only about the next test. Look at the whole portfolio. A healthy backlog mixes quick fixes, medium tests and a few bold bets. If every item at the top is a small copy tweak, you'll get small, hard-to-detect effects and a programme that feels busy but changes little. If every item is a bold redesign, you'll run very few tests and learn slowly. Aim for balance across pages and mechanisms too, so you're not testing the same idea on the same page again and again. Review the mix each month, and deliberately promote one bolder idea when the portfolio gets too timid.
```

Scene 15 — Recap

```text
Recap. Prioritisation makes trade-offs visible and tames opinions. ICE and PIE are fast but subjective. Evidence-weighted scoring uses factual yes-or-no questions, so ideas with evidence rise. Always check feasibility against traffic. Use AI to flag gaps, not to invent confidence. Try this now: put your backlog in a CSV with the columns from the lesson text, run the scoring script, and add a feasibility column for your top ten ideas.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Test, build or drop

```text
Here's a question that saves CRO teams months: does this idea actually need an A/B test? Many don't. Some are bugs you should just fix. Some are too small to measure with your traffic. Some are better tested as prototypes, or in ad platforms. And some should simply be dropped. In this lecture you'll learn a decision tree for test, build or drop, alternatives when traffic is low, how to build safely with feature flags and staged rollouts, and a feasibility check you can run in seconds.
```

Scene 2 — Why the decision matters

```text
Why does this decision matter? Because testing has costs. It takes development time, traffic and weeks of waiting. If you test a broken checkout fix, you're deliberately showing half your visitors a broken checkout for a month. That's not rigour. It's waste. On the other hand, if you ship a big redesign without testing, you might never know it quietly hurt conversion. The skill is choosing the right route for each idea, so testing capacity goes where it adds the most value.
```

Scene 3 — The decision tree

```text
Here's the decision tree. First: is it a bug, a broken experience or a legal requirement? Just do it, then monitor. Second: is it testable with your traffic, meaning you can reach the sample size for a worthwhile effect in about six weeks? If not, look for another route. Third: is the risk or uncertainty high, like a pricing change or a redesign? Then test, if feasible. Fourth: is the evidence strong and the risk low? Build it with monitoring. And if the evidence is weak and the idea isn't important, drop it or park it for more research.
```

Scene 4 — The categories

```text
Let's define the categories. Just do it: bugs, broken links, failing payment methods, accessibility failures, legal requirements. Nobody needs proof that a broken checkout is bad. Test: changes with real uncertainty and enough traffic, like a new pricing page layout or a different offer. Build: changes supported by strong evidence where testing isn't feasible or worthwhile, released with monitoring. Research more: promising ideas with thin evidence. And drop: ideas with no evidence and little potential. Dropping is a decision, not a failure.
```

Scene 5 — Simple example: Lake District hotel

```text
A simple example. A boutique hotel in the Lake District gets about three thousand website visits a month and a handful of direct bookings a week. Three ideas are on the table. The booking widget fails on some mobile browsers: just do it. Adding a clear cancellation policy near the booking button, supported by guest emails asking about it: build, and monitor bookings. And a complete homepage redesign: far too little traffic to test, so they run five quick prototype tests with past guests first. Nothing here needs a classic A/B test, and all three decisions are sound.
```

Scene 6 — When a test is worth it

```text
When is a test really worth it? Tests earn their cost when three things are true. The change carries real uncertainty, meaning smart people genuinely disagree about whether it will help. The potential impact matters to the business, because it sits on a high-value page or affects many customers. And the page has enough traffic to detect a worthwhile effect in a reasonable time. Pricing presentation, offer structure, checkout flow changes and major layout changes on key templates often meet all three. A wording tweak on a low-traffic page rarely does. Save your testing capacity for decisions where being wrong would be expensive.
```

Scene 7 — Low-traffic alternatives

```text
Low traffic isn't a dead end. It just changes the tools. Fix bugs and monitor. Build changes backed by strong qualitative evidence, using staged rollouts. Use prototype usability tests for big structural changes. Test messages and angles in ad or email platforms, where the sample is larger. Test bolder changes that could produce bigger effects, because big effects need smaller samples. And be patient. Sometimes the right answer is park it until you have more evidence.
```

Scene 8 — Realistic example: Riyadh skincare (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic scenario with illustrative numbers. A skincare brand in Riyadh wants to test a new product page layout. The page converts at three percent, and the team wants to detect a ten percent relative improvement. At thirty thousand eligible visitors a week, that's around four weeks: a test. But their second idea is for a niche product page with five thousand visitors a week. The same maths says around twenty-one weeks. Not a test. Instead, they build the change behind a feature flag, roll it out to ten percent, then fifty, then everyone, watching errors, page speed and conversion. And they keep a small holdback group on the old version for a few weeks to estimate impact.
```

Scene 9 — Watch me do it: feasibility check

```text
Watch me run the feasibility check. I reuse the sample size function from the statistics module. I write a small helper: give it the baseline conversion rate, the minimum worthwhile effect, weekly visitors, the number of variants, and a maximum number of weeks I'm willing to run, say six. It calculates the sample per variant, converts it into weeks, and returns either test, with the expected duration, or not a test here, with the options. I run it for thirty thousand visitors a week: test, about four weeks. For five thousand: not a test, about twenty-one weeks. It takes seconds, and it ends a lot of arguments.
```

Scene 10 — Building safely

```text
Build doesn't mean forget. Use release practices from product teams. Feature flags, available in tools like GrowthBook, PostHog, LaunchDarkly, Optimizely and Statsig, let you switch a change on for a percentage of users and off instantly if something breaks. Staged rollouts, from ten to fifty to a hundred percent, catch bugs and speed regressions. Holdbacks keep a small share of users on the old experience to estimate impact. And annotate your analytics with the release date, so anyone analysing later knows what changed and when.
```

Scene 11 — Decision records

```text
Record every decision. A short decision record for each idea: the hypothesis, the route you chose, test, build, drop, research or just do it, and why. The expected effect and what you'll monitor. The date. And later, what actually happened. This turns your backlog into a learning library, even for ideas you never tested. It also helps new team members understand why things are the way they are.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Testing bug fixes. Testing on pages that could never reach significance. Shipping big redesigns without any research. Building without monitoring. Never dropping anything, so the backlog grows forever. And treating build as a lesser choice. For many businesses, well-researched builds with good monitoring are the majority of CRO work.
```

Scene 13 — Recap

```text
Recap. Not every idea needs an A/B test. Just do it for bugs and legal requirements. Test when uncertainty is real and traffic allows. Build strong-evidence changes with flags, staged rollouts, holdbacks and monitoring. Research more when evidence is thin, and drop what doesn't earn its place. Try this now: run the feasibility helper from the lesson text on your top five backlog ideas, assign each a route, and write a one-line decision record for each.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Landing page anatomy and message match

```text
A visitor clicks your ad. The page loads. In the next few seconds, their brain answers one question: am I in the right place? If the answer is no, they're gone, and you paid for the click anyway. In this lecture you'll learn what a landing page must do, why message match matters even more when ad platforms assemble ads automatically, the building blocks of a strong page, visual hierarchy, and why above the fold is a myth that won't die. Then you'll watch me run a message-match audit across three traffic sources.
```

Scene 2 — Why anatomy matters

```text
Why does anatomy matter? Because landing pages sit where spend becomes revenue. Every paid channel sends traffic somewhere, and small differences in how well that page does its job multiply across every click. A page that confirms relevance instantly, answers the key questions and makes the next step easy can make the same ad budget go much further. A generic page wastes it quietly, click after click.
```

Scene 3 — Four jobs

```text
A landing page has four jobs. Confirm relevance: you're in the right place. Communicate value: here's what you get and why it matters. Build trust: here's why you can believe it. And enable action: here's exactly what to do next. Think of a hotel reception. Within seconds, you know you're in the right hotel, what's available, that it's legitimate, and where to sign in. A good landing page is a good reception desk.
```

Scene 4 — Message match

```text
Message match is the first job. The headline should echo the promise that brought people there: the ad's words, the search query, the email subject. Match the offer, the visual, and even the product shown. And here's the modern twist. Performance Max, AI Max for Search and Meta's Advantage+ mix headlines and creative, and some generate ad text from your landing page. So one page may receive many different promises, and your page copy becomes ad input. Check the asset and search-term reports monthly, and build dedicated pages for major themes.
```

Scene 5 — Building blocks

```text
The building blocks. A headline that matches and states the value. A subheadline that explains how and for whom. A primary call to action, repeated where it helps. Proof near the claims, like reviews, ratings, logos and guarantees. A visual that shows the product or outcome, not generic stock photos. Objection handling, like delivery, returns, pricing and security. And a focused layout, often with reduced navigation on dedicated landing templates. Every block should earn its place by answering a question the visitor has.
```

Scene 6 — Visual hierarchy

```text
Visual hierarchy guides the eye. The most important element should be the most prominent: usually the headline, then the product visual, then the call to action. Use contrast, size and space deliberately. One primary button colour, used only for primary actions. Enough white space that the key message breathes. And watch out for auto-rotating carousels in the hero. They move content away before people can read it, and they push your real message out of view. They come up again and again in heuristic reviews.
```

Scene 7 — The 'above the fold' myth

```text
Now the myth. Everything important must be above the fold. Partly true: the first screen must confirm relevance and make the next step clear. But people scroll, especially on mobile, if the first screen gives them a reason to. Cramming everything into the first screen makes it cluttered and less clear. The real rule: the first screen must earn the scroll. Then the rest of the page answers the next questions, in order.
```

Scene 8 — Simple example: Lahore creator course

```text
A simple example. A creator in Lahore sells an online course on mobile video editing. Her Instagram ads promise: edit reels on your phone in one weekend. The landing page opens with: welcome to my academy, and a photo of her office. After the rewrite, the headline matches the ad: edit scroll-stopping reels on your phone, in one weekend. A short clip shows a before-and-after edit. Below it, proof from past students with permission, the syllabus, the price with an instalment option, and one call to action: join the weekend course.
```

Scene 9 — Realistic example: Dubai linen shirts (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic scenario with illustrative details. A fashion retailer in Dubai sends Meta ads for breathable linen shirts to its homepage, which has a five-banner carousel, a nine-item menu and welcome to our store. The agency builds a dedicated landing template. The hero matches the ad: linen shirts for forty-degree summers, breathable and relaxed fit. The subheadline covers the two top objections from research: free exchanges on size, delivery in two to three days across the UAE. There's a live rating and review count, a reduced menu, and the product grid starts immediately below. They A/B test it against the homepage for that campaign, with revenue per visitor as the primary metric.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me do it: message-match audit

```text
Watch me run a message-match audit. I make a table with one row per traffic source or ad group. Columns: the top three query themes or ad angles, the promise in the ad, the landing page headline, the proof on the first screen, whether they match, and the fix. Row one: search ads for emergency plumber Leeds, promising arrival within sixty minutes. The page headline says plumbing services in Yorkshire, and there's no proof on the first screen. No match. Fix: new headline with the time promise and reviews. Row two: Meta ads for the Eid collection, promising delivery before Eid. The page says new arrivals. Partial match. Fix: an Eid headline and the delivery cut-off date. The table takes an hour and usually reveals the fastest wins in the account.
```

Scene 11 — AI page builders

```text
AI page builders can generate a complete landing page from a prompt. They're useful for speed, but they don't know your research. Start from your message hierarchy and your research findings. Then check the output against this lesson's anatomy: one goal, a matched headline, visible proof, a clear call to action, objection handling, and fast mobile loading. Delete generic sections the tool adds, like our mission or a second call to action. And test it on a real phone with a real mobile connection.
```

Scene 12 — Speed is anatomy

```text
Speed is part of anatomy too. A beautiful landing page that takes five seconds to show its headline on a mid-range phone has already lost many of the visitors you paid for, especially from social apps, where people are one tap away from going back to their feed. Check the page on a real mid-range Android phone with a normal mobile connection, not the office wifi. Look at Largest Contentful Paint for the hero, and watch for layout shifts that move the button just as someone taps. Common culprits are oversized hero images, auto-playing background videos, heavy fonts and too many third-party scripts, including testing and chat tools. Treat every script on a landing template as a cost that must justify itself.
```

Scene 13 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Sending paid traffic to the homepage. Headlines that don't match the ad. Hero carousels. Generic stock images. Multiple competing calls to action. Proof buried at the bottom. Slow mobile loading. And never checking what automated ad formats are actually promising on your behalf.
```

Scene 14 — Recap

```text
Recap. A landing page confirms relevance, communicates value, builds trust and enables action. Message match comes first, and it matters more than ever now that ad platforms assemble and generate ads. Build pages from clear blocks, guide the eye with hierarchy, and make the first screen earn the scroll. Try this now: build the message-match audit table from the lesson text for your top five traffic sources or ad groups, and fix the worst mismatch this week.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — UX and copy heuristics

```text
You don't always need months of research to find problems. Experienced reviewers can spot many conversion issues in an hour, by looking through the right lenses. Those lenses are called heuristics. In this lecture you'll learn a conversion heuristic framework, copy and UX heuristics that often matter, mobile-specific checks, and newer heuristics for accessibility, consent banners, localisation and AI chat widgets. Then you'll watch me run an AI-assisted heuristic pre-review, and verify it on a real phone.
```

Scene 2 — Why heuristics

```text
Why use heuristics? Because they're fast, cheap and surprisingly effective at finding obvious problems. They give you a structured way to review a page, so you don't just react to personal taste. But remember what they are: lenses, not laws. A heuristic suggests where a problem might be. Research and testing confirm whether it matters for your audience. Use heuristic findings as one input to your research log, marked with lower confidence than findings from real user behaviour.
```

Scene 3 — Six lenses

```text
Here's a conversion heuristic framework you can use on any page. Relevance: does the page match what the visitor expected? Clarity: is the value proposition and next step instantly clear? Motivation: does the page give strong reasons to act? Friction: what makes the action harder than it needs to be? Anxiety: what doubts about trust, security, delivery or returns are unanswered? And distraction: what pulls attention away from the goal? Score each page on each lens, and note specific evidence.
```

Scene 4 — Copy heuristics

```text
Copy heuristics. Clarity beats cleverness: can a visitor explain what you sell after five seconds? Specific beats vague: numbers, names and details. Benefits and outcomes beat features alone. Customer language beats internal jargon. And every claim should be supported by proof nearby. On pricing pages, check that prices, taxes and what's included are unambiguous, and that the most common questions are answered right there.
```

Scene 5 — UX heuristics

```text
UX heuristics that often matter. Visible, consistent navigation and search. Obvious primary actions. Forms that ask only for what's needed. Clear feedback after every action. Error prevention before error messages. Consistent patterns across pages. And the classics, like Jakob Nielsen's ten usability heuristics, such as visibility of system status, matching the real world, and recognition rather than recall, remain excellent checklists.
```

Scene 6 — Mobile heuristics

```text
Mobile deserves its own pass. Tap targets big enough and far enough apart. Text readable without zooming. Sticky calls to action that don't cover content. Forms using the right keyboard type, like a number pad for phone numbers. Key information visible without horizontal scrolling. And watch the things that pile up at the bottom of the screen: cookie banners, chat launchers and app download prompts. On a small phone, they can cover the very button you want people to tap.
```

Scene 7 — Newer heuristics

```text
Some heuristics are newer. Accessibility: contrast, focus visibility, target size, labels and error identification, following WCAG two point two. In the EU, the European Accessibility Act has applied to many ecommerce services since June twenty twenty-five. Consent and privacy UX: is reject as easy as accept, where required, and does the banner hide your call to action? Localisation: right-to-left layouts for Arabic and Urdu, local currency, dates and payment methods. And AI features: chat widgets that cover calls to action, or AI answers that contradict your own prices and policies.
```

Scene 8 — Simple example: London SaaS pricing page

```text
A simple example. A software company's pricing page in London has three plans with fourteen feature rows each, no recommended plan, prices that don't say whether VAT is included, and the FAQ below the fold. Running the six lenses: clarity fails, because the differences are buried; anxiety fails, because VAT and cancellation are unclear; and friction is high, because comparing is hard work. The fixes: highlight a recommended plan for the main segment, show five key differences first with the rest expandable, state VAT clearly, and put four pricing FAQs directly below.
```

Scene 9 — Realistic example: Jeddah online pharmacy (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic scenario with illustrative details. An online pharmacy in Jeddah runs a heuristic review on its mobile product pages. The review finds that the chat widget covers the add-to-cart button on smaller phones, the prescription requirement is only explained after checkout starts, and the Arabic layout mirrors some icons incorrectly, like a back arrow pointing forward. None of these needed an A/B test to diagnose. Two are just-do-it fixes. The prescription explanation becomes a hypothesis: explaining the prescription process on the product page will reduce checkout abandonment for prescription items.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me do it: AI heuristic pre-review

```text
Watch me run an AI-assisted pre-review. I take mobile screenshots of the product page, the cart and the first checkout step. I give a multimodal assistant the context: the audience, the traffic source and the goal. I ask it to review against the six lenses plus accessibility, and for each issue to give the screenshot, the element, the lens, why it might hurt conversion, a suggested change and its confidence. And I tell it not to guess about anything it can't see, but to list those as needs checking. Then I pick up a real phone and check every issue. I remove two false positives, and add three things the model couldn't see: a slow response when tapping the size selector, an error state, and how a screen reader announces the price.
```

Scene 11 — Running a heuristic review

```text
How do you run a heuristic review properly? Define the page, audience, traffic source and goal first. Review on the devices your visitors use, mostly mobile. Go lens by lens rather than all at once. Ideally, two or three reviewers work independently, then merge findings. Record each issue with its location, the lens, the evidence, and severity. And treat the output as hypotheses and quick fixes, not as proven problems. Heuristic findings that line up with analytics or recordings deserve higher priority.
```

Scene 12 — Severity makes it actionable

```text
Once you have a list, score severity so something actually gets fixed. A simple scale works. Blocker: stops people completing the task, like a button that doesn't work on a common phone. Major: causes real hesitation or errors for many people, like unclear delivery costs. Minor: an annoyance, like inconsistent capitalisation. Then add reach: how many visitors hit this page or element? A major issue on the checkout outranks a major issue on the careers page. Blockers usually become just-do-it fixes. Majors become hypotheses or builds. Minors get batched into a monthly tidy-up. That way, a two-hour review turns into a short, prioritised plan instead of a long document nobody reads.
```

Scene 13 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Treating heuristics as laws. Reviewing on a desktop monitor when most visitors use phones. Mixing personal taste with heuristic findings. Trusting AI reviews without checking on real devices. Ignoring accessibility and localisation. And producing a hundred-item list with no severity, so nothing gets fixed.
```

Scene 14 — Recap

```text
Recap. Heuristics are fast lenses: relevance, clarity, motivation, friction, anxiety and distraction, plus copy, UX, mobile, accessibility, consent, localisation and AI-widget checks. Use them to find likely problems and quick fixes, then confirm with research. AI can speed up a pre-review, but a human verifies on real devices. Try this now: run the six-lens review on your highest-traffic mobile page, using the AI prompt from the lesson text as a first pass, and verify every issue on a real phone before logging it.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Optimising forms and checkout

```text
The checkout is where intent meets friction. The customer has chosen a product, decided to buy, and started paying. And yet many of them leave. Every unnecessary field, every surprise fee, every payment method that fails on their phone, is a customer you'd already won and then lost. In this lecture you'll learn form optimisation principles, checkout best practices for twenty twenty-six, local payment methods across the UK, the Gulf and Pakistan, and how to balance lead quantity with quality. Then you'll watch me run a full checkout audit on a phone.
```

Scene 2 — Why forms and checkout

```text
Why focus here? Because checkout and form fixes are often the highest-return work in CRO. The visitor has already shown intent. You don't need to persuade them to want the product. You need to stop getting in their way. Removing a single confusing step, adding the wallet they use every day, or making the total cost clear from the start can recover sales that were already yours.
```

Scene 3 — Form principles

```text
Form principles. Ask only for what you need now, and mark any optional fields. Use one column. Keep labels visible above fields, not as disappearing placeholders. Use the right input types, so phones show a number pad for phone numbers. Set autofill attributes so browsers can fill names and addresses. Validate inline after someone leaves a field, with specific, friendly messages. And explain why you need sensitive information, like a phone number for delivery updates only.
```

Scene 4 — Multi-step forms

```text
Multi-step forms can help when a form is long, as long as each step feels easy and progress is visible. Start with the easiest questions to build momentum, group related fields, and show a progress indicator. For lead forms, consider progressive profiling: ask the essentials now and more later, once trust exists. And always measure quality alongside quantity. A shorter form can double submissions and halve the share that sales accepts.
```

Scene 5 — Checkout best practice

```text
Checkout best practices for twenty twenty-six. Offer guest checkout, with account creation offered after purchase. Show express wallets early: Apple Pay, Google Pay, Shop Pay on Shopify stores, PayPal. Make the total cost clear before checkout: delivery cost or free-delivery threshold, and taxes. In the UK, mandatory fees must be in the headline price under the rules on drip pricing. Show delivery dates, not just three to five days. Keep the order summary visible and editable. And put a returns summary one tap away.
```

Scene 6 — Local payments

```text
Payments are local. In Saudi Arabia, many shoppers expect mada debit cards, and buy-now-pay-later services like Tabby and Tamara are widely used in the Gulf. In Pakistan, cash on delivery remains important, alongside mobile wallets like JazzCash and Easypaisa. In the UK, cards, wallets and buy-now-pay-later options like Klarna and Clearpay are common. Offer only the methods you can support properly. If you show instalments, show the full price, the provider and the terms, with no pressure. And test 3D Secure authentication on mobile browsers and in-app browsers, where pop-ups can fail.
```

Scene 7 — Addresses and phones

```text
Addresses deserve special attention. Autocomplete saves typing, but always provide a manual fallback. And remember that many areas don't use postcodes the way the UK does. In parts of the Gulf and Pakistan, delivery depends on building names, areas and landmarks. A form that demands a postcode, or rejects an address without one, loses real customers. Phone fields should accept local and international formats, like zero five or plus nine seven one, and zero three or plus nine two.
```

Scene 8 — Simple example: Manchester home fragrance

```text
A simple example. A home-fragrance store in Manchester notices many shoppers leave at the delivery step. The delivery cost appears only there, and the free-delivery threshold isn't mentioned anywhere earlier. The fix: show the threshold in the header and cart, with a progress message like you're eight pounds away from free delivery, and show the delivery cost estimate in the cart. No surprise, no drip. Abandonment at that step becomes a metric to watch after the change.
```

Scene 9 — Realistic example: Riyadh electronics (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic scenario with illustrative details. An electronics retailer in Riyadh finds that mobile checkout completion is much lower for customers arriving from social media apps. Recordings show payment failing inside in-app browsers, where the bank's authentication pop-up doesn't load properly. The address form also requires a postcode, and the phone field rejects numbers starting with plus nine six six. The team fixes the authentication flow using the payment provider's recommended mobile integration, makes the postcode optional, and accepts both local and international phone formats. They also move mada and Apple Pay to the top of the payment options. These are just-do-it fixes, monitored by segment afterwards.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me do it: checkout audit

```text
Watch me run the checkout audit. I use a real phone, and I go through checkout as a guest, in each market we serve. Entry: is there guest checkout, are wallets shown early, and is the total cost clear before I start? Forms: only essential fields, autocomplete with manual fallback, correct keyboards, inline validation, and local phone formats. Payment and trust: are local methods supported properly, is buy-now-pay-later shown with the full price and terms, does authentication work in in-app browsers, and do declined payments say you haven't been charged? Delivery and policies: real dates, event cut-offs, returns one tap away. Accessibility and performance: screen reader, errors announced, no layout shift near buttons. And tracking: each checkout event fires once.
```

Scene 11 — Accessibility and compliance

```text
Accessibility and compliance matter here more than anywhere. Checkout must work with a keyboard and a screen reader, with visible labels and errors that are announced. The European Accessibility Act has applied to many ecommerce services in the EU since June twenty twenty-five. Consent for marketing must be separate and unticked, where the law requires it. And subscription sign-ups need clear renewal terms and easy cancellation. Accessible checkouts are better checkouts for everyone.
```

Scene 12 — The discount code field

```text
Let's talk about the discount code field, because it's a quiet conversion leak. When shoppers see an empty box labelled promo code right before paying, many think, am I paying more than I should? Some leave to search for codes, and not all of them come back. Others find a code on a coupon site, which erodes your margin. Options include: showing the field as a small text link rather than an open box, applying automatic discounts for active promotions so nobody needs a code, and using unique, single-use codes for partners and creators. If you do change it, measure revenue per visitor and margin, not just conversion, because code usage affects both.
```

Scene 13 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Forced account creation. Surprise fees late in checkout. Too many fields. Placeholder-only labels. Postcode fields that reject real addresses. Missing local payment methods. Untested in-app browsers. Discount code fields that send people off to search for codes. And shorter lead forms judged only on submissions.
```

Scene 14 — Recap

```text
Recap. Forms and checkouts are where intent meets friction, so remove friction relentlessly. Ask only for what you need, use the right inputs, and validate kindly. Offer guest checkout, express wallets and the local payment methods your customers actually use, and make the total cost clear from the start. Test authentication in in-app browsers, adapt address forms to local reality, and build in accessibility. For lead forms, measure quality alongside volume. Try this now: run the checkout audit checklist from the lesson text on a real phone for each market you serve, and fix every red item that's a bug before you test anything else.
```

</details>

### 14. A/B test fundamentals and experiment design

Lesson `ab-test-fundamentals` · module “A/B testing statistics and experimentation platforms” · target 9 min · 14 scenes · 1203 words · planned 8:42 (words ≈ 8:43) · status: **to produce**

| Term | Say |
|---|---|
| SRM | S R M |
| CDN | C D N |
| RTL | right to left |
| QA | Q A |

| # | Time | Sec | Narration | On screen | Visual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0:00 | 42 | A test says your new product page lifts conversion by twelve percent. Before you celebrate, can you prove the test itself was sound? Most bad decisions in experimentation don't come from wrong maths. They come from broken tests: uneven splits, flickering variants, missing tracking, overlapping experiments. In this lecture you'll learn what an A/B test really does, the types of tests, how to write a test plan, how to choose the unit of randomisation, and how to catch sample ratio mismatch and flicker. Then you'll watch me check a split with a few lines of Python. | A/B test fundamentals<br>• Sound tests before sound maths<br>• Types of tests and test plans<br>• Randomisation units<br>• SRM, flicker and QA | Layout: a results card shows '+12%' with confetti; the confetti freezes and a magnifying glass inspects the test's plumbing: pipes labelled split, tracking, flicker. Bullets build on the right. Timing (42s): title 0-3s; 4 bullets build in narration order about every 8s; final 5s hold on the key line. |
| 2 | 0:42 | 30 | Why is design so important? Because an A/B test is a measuring instrument. If the instrument is broken, the measurement is meaningless, however impressive it looks. Randomisation is what makes the instrument work. It creates two groups that are the same in every way except the change, so seasonality, campaigns, news and competitor moves affect both equally. Break the randomisation, even slightly, and you're back to comparing apples with oranges. | Why design matters<br>• A test is a measuring instrument<br>• Randomisation makes groups comparable<br>• Break it and results mean nothing | Layout: a set of weighing scales; identical crowds of people icons split randomly onto each side; an external event (a sale banner) affects both sides equally. Then a broken scale shows unequal crowds. Timing (30s): title 0-3s; 3 bullets build in narration order about every 7s; final 5s hold on the key line. |
| 3 | 1:12 | 35 | Types of tests. A/B: control versus one variant, used for most tests. A/B/n: several variants, which needs more traffic because each variant gets a smaller share. Multivariate tests combine several elements and need very high traffic. Split URL tests send people to different pages, useful for big redesigns. And server-side or feature-flag tests change logic in your code, which suits pricing, search, checkout logic and apps. Every extra variant splits traffic further, so keep variants few unless traffic is large. | Types of tests<br>• A/B and A/B/n<br>• Multivariate<br>• Split URL<br>• Server-side / feature flags | Layout: five icons in a row; A/B splits traffic in two; A/B/n splits into four thinner streams; a multivariate grid; two URLs; a code switch icon for server-side. Timing (35s): title 0-3s; 4 bullets build in narration order about every 7s; final 5s hold on the key line. |
| 4 | 1:47 | 31 | Write the plan before you build. Test name, hypothesis and audience. Allocation, usually fifty-fifty. The primary metric, secondary metrics and guardrails. The minimum detectable effect, the sample size and duration, covering at least full weeks. Stop rules: stop early only for guardrail harm or a broken experience. And a QA checklist. The plan is your contract with future you. It stops you from changing the question after you've seen the answer. | The test plan<br>• Hypothesis, audience, allocation<br>• Primary, secondary, guardrails<br>• MDE, sample size, full weeks<br>• Stop rules + QA | Layout: a test-plan document with fields filling in; a signature line at the bottom labelled 'agreed before launch' gets signed. Timing (31s): title 0-3s; 4 bullets build in narration order about every 6s; final 5s hold on the key line. |
| 5 | 2:18 | 47 | Let's talk about metrics inside the plan, because the plan is where most tests are won or lost. Choose one primary metric that decides the outcome, usually revenue per visitor or purchase conversion for a store, qualified leads for a service. Add a few secondary metrics that explain how the change worked, like add-to-cart rate or checkout starts. And add guardrails that must not get worse: return rate, page speed, support contacts, unsubscribes. Then write the decision rule in one sentence, before launch. For example: ship B if revenue per visitor improves at our planned confidence and no guardrail worsens meaningfully. That single sentence prevents most post-test arguments. | Metrics in the plan<br>• One primary metric decides<br>• Secondary metrics explain<br>• Guardrails must not worsen<br>• One-sentence decision rule | Layout: a plan card with three metric slots filling (primary large, secondary medium, guardrails small with red zones); a decision-rule sentence types across the bottom and locks. Timing (47s): title 0-3s; 4 bullets build in narration order about every 10s; final 5s hold on the key line. |
| 6 | 3:05 | 33 | Unit of randomisation. Most web tests randomise by user, using a cookie or a logged-in user ID, so each person sees a consistent experience every visit. Randomising by page view would show someone different versions on each click, which is confusing and statistically messy. For logged-in products, user-ID assignment works across devices. And think about consent: where consent is required before storing identifiers, the tool may only run for consenting users, so your results describe that group. | Unit of randomisation<br>• Usually the user, not the page view<br>• Consistent experience per person<br>• User ID across devices<br>• Consent changes who is in the test | Layout: a person icon visits three times; with user-level randomisation they see B each time; with page-view randomisation the version flickers A, B, A with a confused face. Timing (33s): title 0-3s; 4 bullets build in narration order about every 6s; final 5s hold on the key line. |
| 7 | 3:38 | 40 | Now sample ratio mismatch. If you allocate fifty-fifty but see fifty thousand six hundred users in A and forty-nine thousand four hundred in B, something may be wrong. A redirect losing users, a bot filter hitting one variant, or a bug stopping the variant loading on some browsers. A simple chi-square test tells you how likely that imbalance is by chance. If it's very unlikely, commonly a p-value below one in a thousand, the test is invalid until you explain and fix it. Don't read the results first. Check the split first. | Sample ratio mismatch (SRM)<br>• Planned 50/50, observed imbalance<br>• Causes: redirects, bots, bugs<br>• Chi-square check; strict threshold<br>• Check the split before results | Layout: two bars labelled A and B with counts 50,600 and 49,400; a warning light turns red with 'p ≈ 0.00015'; a checklist of causes appears beside it. Timing (40s): title 0-3s; 4 bullets build in narration order about every 8s; final 5s hold on the key line. |
| 8 | 4:18 | 37 | Two more threats. Flicker: client-side tools change the page after it starts loading, so the original can flash before the variant appears, which biases results and annoys users. Mitigate by loading the script correctly with anti-flicker handling, keeping variant code light, or testing server-side or at the edge for big changes. And QA: check every variant on key devices, browsers and languages, including right-to-left layouts; confirm tracking fires for all metrics; check the initial split; and make sure no other tests conflict on the same pages. | Flicker and QA<br>• Original flashes before variant<br>• Anti-flicker, light code, server-side<br>• QA: devices, languages, tracking<br>• No conflicting tests | Layout: a page loads showing the old headline for a split second, then snaps to the new one with a 'flicker' label. Then a QA checklist ticks devices, RTL, tracking, split and conflicts. Timing (37s): title 0-3s; 4 bullets build in narration order about every 7s; final 5s hold on the key line. |
| 9 | 4:55 | 34 | A simple example of an invalid test. An online store tests a new product page, and after two weeks the variant is winning strongly. The SRM check fails: the variant has noticeably fewer users than expected. Investigation shows the variant breaks on an older mobile browser, so those users, who convert poorly anyway, dropped out of the variant's data but not the control's. The win was an artefact. After fixing the bug and restarting, the effect is much smaller. | Simple example: an invalid 'win'<br>• Variant winning strongly<br>• SRM check fails<br>• Variant broke on an old browser<br>• After the fix, a much smaller effect | Layout: a big winning bar for B shrinks after a bug icon is revealed on an old phone; a restart icon runs the test again and a modest bar remains. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — A/B test fundamentals

```text
A test says your new product page lifts conversion by twelve percent. Before you celebrate, can you prove the test itself was sound? Most bad decisions in experimentation don't come from wrong maths. They come from broken tests: uneven splits, flickering variants, missing tracking, overlapping experiments. In this lecture you'll learn what an A/B test really does, the types of tests, how to write a test plan, how to choose the unit of randomisation, and how to catch sample ratio mismatch and flicker. Then you'll watch me check a split with a few lines of Python.
```

Scene 2 — Why design matters

```text
Why is design so important? Because an A/B test is a measuring instrument. If the instrument is broken, the measurement is meaningless, however impressive it looks. Randomisation is what makes the instrument work. It creates two groups that are the same in every way except the change, so seasonality, campaigns, news and competitor moves affect both equally. Break the randomisation, even slightly, and you're back to comparing apples with oranges.
```

Scene 3 — Types of tests

```text
Types of tests. A/B: control versus one variant, used for most tests. A/B/n: several variants, which needs more traffic because each variant gets a smaller share. Multivariate tests combine several elements and need very high traffic. Split URL tests send people to different pages, useful for big redesigns. And server-side or feature-flag tests change logic in your code, which suits pricing, search, checkout logic and apps. Every extra variant splits traffic further, so keep variants few unless traffic is large.
```

Scene 4 — The test plan

```text
Write the plan before you build. Test name, hypothesis and audience. Allocation, usually fifty-fifty. The primary metric, secondary metrics and guardrails. The minimum detectable effect, the sample size and duration, covering at least full weeks. Stop rules: stop early only for guardrail harm or a broken experience. And a QA checklist. The plan is your contract with future you. It stops you from changing the question after you've seen the answer.
```

Scene 5 — Metrics in the plan

```text
Let's talk about metrics inside the plan, because the plan is where most tests are won or lost. Choose one primary metric that decides the outcome, usually revenue per visitor or purchase conversion for a store, qualified leads for a service. Add a few secondary metrics that explain how the change worked, like add-to-cart rate or checkout starts. And add guardrails that must not get worse: return rate, page speed, support contacts, unsubscribes. Then write the decision rule in one sentence, before launch. For example: ship B if revenue per visitor improves at our planned confidence and no guardrail worsens meaningfully. That single sentence prevents most post-test arguments.
```

Scene 6 — Unit of randomisation

```text
Unit of randomisation. Most web tests randomise by user, using a cookie or a logged-in user ID, so each person sees a consistent experience every visit. Randomising by page view would show someone different versions on each click, which is confusing and statistically messy. For logged-in products, user-ID assignment works across devices. And think about consent: where consent is required before storing identifiers, the tool may only run for consenting users, so your results describe that group.
```

Scene 7 — Sample ratio mismatch (SRM)

```text
Now sample ratio mismatch. If you allocate fifty-fifty but see fifty thousand six hundred users in A and forty-nine thousand four hundred in B, something may be wrong. A redirect losing users, a bot filter hitting one variant, or a bug stopping the variant loading on some browsers. A simple chi-square test tells you how likely that imbalance is by chance. If it's very unlikely, commonly a p-value below one in a thousand, the test is invalid until you explain and fix it. Don't read the results first. Check the split first.
```

Scene 8 — Flicker and QA

```text
Two more threats. Flicker: client-side tools change the page after it starts loading, so the original can flash before the variant appears, which biases results and annoys users. Mitigate by loading the script correctly with anti-flicker handling, keeping variant code light, or testing server-side or at the edge for big changes. And QA: check every variant on key devices, browsers and languages, including right-to-left layouts; confirm tracking fires for all metrics; check the initial split; and make sure no other tests conflict on the same pages.
```

Scene 9 — Simple example: an invalid 'win'

```text
A simple example of an invalid test. An online store tests a new product page, and after two weeks the variant is winning strongly. The SRM check fails: the variant has noticeably fewer users than expected. Investigation shows the variant breaks on an older mobile browser, so those users, who convert poorly anyway, dropped out of the variant's data but not the control's. The win was an artefact. After fixing the bug and restarting, the effect is much smaller.
```

Scene 10 — Realistic example: Dubai travel checkout (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic scenario with illustrative details. A travel company in Dubai runs a checkout test using a client-side tool. Results look flat, and the SRM check passes. But QA had missed something: the variant's layout broke in Arabic, where the right-to-left version pushed the payment button below a sticky banner. Arabic-language users in the variant struggled, dragging the overall result down. The team fixes the layout, adds right-to-left checks to their QA template, and reruns. The lesson: SRM catches missing users, but only QA catches broken experiences for users who are still there.
```

Scene 11 — Watch me do it: SRM check in Python

```text
Watch me check a split. I have user counts per variant from our data warehouse. I write a tiny function using Python's standard library: it computes the chi-square statistic for the observed split against the expected fifty-fifty and converts it to a p-value. I run it on two tests. Fifty thousand two hundred and ten versus forty-nine thousand seven hundred and ninety gives a p-value of about zero point one eight. Fine. Fifty thousand six hundred versus forty-nine thousand four hundred gives about zero point zero zero zero one five. That's a mismatch. A one point two percent imbalance looks tiny, but on a hundred thousand users it's very unlikely to be chance. So before anyone opens the results dashboard, I start investigating.
```

Scene 12 — Choosing an approach

```text
Choosing a testing approach. Client-side tools with visual editors let marketers launch copy and layout tests quickly, but they add page weight and flicker, and they can break when the site changes. Server-side testing and feature flags need developers, but handle pricing, search and checkout logic without flicker. Edge testing, at the CDN, serves full-page variants before HTML reaches the browser. Many mature programmes use both client-side and server-side. Whatever you choose, make sure your analytics can see which variant each user received, so you can verify results independently of the tool.
```

Scene 13 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. No written plan. Overlapping tests on the same element. Ignoring SRM warnings. Skipping cross-device and right-to-left QA. Too many variants for the traffic. Letting consent settings silently change who's in the test without documenting it. And trusting the tool's dashboard without being able to verify assignments in your own data.
```

Scene 14 — Recap

```text
Recap. An A/B test is a measuring instrument that works only if randomisation works. Write the plan first, randomise by user, check the split for sample ratio mismatch before reading results, prevent flicker, and QA every variant across devices and languages. Choose client-side, server-side or edge approaches to suit the change. Try this now: take your last test, pull the user counts per variant, run the SRM function from the lesson text, and add right-to-left and in-app browser checks to your QA template.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Sample size, significance and power

```text
Flip a fair coin a hundred times. Will you get exactly fifty heads? Probably not. You might get forty-six, or fifty-five. Now imagine two identical web pages. Their conversion rates will differ too, purely by chance. So when your variant beats the control, how do you know it's real? That's what this lecture is about. You'll learn the four inputs to every sample size calculation, what significance and power really mean, how to turn sample size into a test duration, and why confidence intervals beat a simple significant or not. Then you'll watch me build a sample size calculator in Python.
```

Scene 2 — Why statistics matter

```text
Why does this matter? Because without statistics, you'll ship noise. You'll celebrate random wins, then wonder why revenue didn't move. Or you'll give up on good ideas because a test was too small to detect them. Getting sample size right before you launch protects you from both. It also tells you, in advance, whether a test is even worth running on a given page, which saves months.
```

Scene 3 — Four inputs

```text
Every sample size calculation has four inputs. The baseline conversion rate: how often the control converts today. The minimum detectable effect: the smallest improvement worth detecting, often expressed as a relative lift, like ten percent. The significance level, alpha: the false positive risk you accept, usually five percent. And statistical power: the chance of detecting a real effect of that size, usually eighty percent. Change any one, and the sample you need changes.
```

Scene 4 — Significance in plain words

```text
Significance in plain language. A result is significant at five percent when a difference this large would happen less than five percent of the time if there were truly no difference. That's what the p-value is. A p-value of zero point zero three means: if the variants were identical, we'd see a difference at least this big about three percent of the time. What it does not mean: there's a ninety-five percent chance the variant is better. And it says nothing about how big or valuable the effect is.
```

Scene 5 — Power in plain words

```text
Power in plain language. Power is the probability that your test detects a real effect of at least the minimum detectable effect. With eighty percent power, if the true uplift is exactly your MDE, you'll detect it about four times out of five, and miss it one time in five. Underpowered tests end inconclusive even when the change genuinely helps. That's why a flat result from a tiny test tells you very little.
```

Scene 6 — The numbers (5% alpha, 80% power)

```text
Here are the numbers, from the full calculation at five percent significance and eighty percent power. They're illustrative planning figures. A three percent baseline and a ten percent relative lift needs about fifty-three thousand visitors per variant. Double the effect to twenty percent, and it drops to about fourteen thousand. A ten percent baseline with a ten percent lift needs about fifteen thousand. But a one percent baseline with a ten percent lift needs over a hundred and sixty thousand per variant. Two lessons. Halving the effect you want to detect roughly quadruples the sample. And low baseline rates need far more traffic.
```

Scene 7 — From sample to duration

```text
Turn sample size into duration. Divide the total sample, all variants together, by your daily eligible visitors. Then round up to whole weeks, so every day of the week is represented, because weekday and weekend behaviour differ. Avoid unusual periods like Eid, White Friday or Black Friday unless that's what you want to learn about. And if the duration comes out longer than about six to eight weeks, reconsider: test a bolder change, use a higher-frequency metric, or test on a larger audience. Long tests suffer from cookie loss and changing conditions.
```

Scene 8 — Simple example: edtech course page (illustrative)

```text
A simple example. A Pakistani edtech site's course page converts at three percent, with about four thousand eligible visitors a day. The team wants to detect a ten percent relative improvement. The calculator says about fifty-three thousand per variant, so a hundred and six thousand in total. At four thousand a day, that's twenty-seven days, rounded up to twenty-eight, four full weeks. That's feasible. If they'd wanted to detect a five percent improvement, it would take roughly four times as long. Too long. So they choose a bolder change worth a ten percent effect.
```

Scene 9 — Realistic example: Riyadh electronics RPV (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic scenario about revenue. An electronics retailer in Riyadh wants revenue per visitor as the primary metric, which is usually better than conversion rate for a store. But revenue has much higher variance: most visitors spend nothing, and a few spend a lot. So the same relative effect needs more traffic. The analyst estimates the standard deviation from historical data, calculates the sample, and finds the test would take ten weeks. Options: test a bolder change, cap extreme order values as a pre-registered rule, or use a variance-reduction method like CUPED, which you'll meet in the next lesson but one. They choose CUPED plus capping, decided before launch.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me do it: the calculator

```text
Watch me build the calculator. I import square root and ceiling from the math module, and NormalDist from statistics, so there's nothing to install. The function takes the baseline, the relative MDE, alpha and power. It validates the inputs, because a baseline of three instead of zero point zero three would give nonsense. It computes the two conversion rates, the z-values for significance and power, and applies the standard two-proportion formula. A second function turns the sample into days, rounded up to whole weeks. I run it for four scenarios, and the printed table matches the numbers you saw earlier. Now I can answer is this test feasible? in seconds, in any planning meeting.
```

Scene 11 — Confidence intervals

```text
Report confidence intervals, not just significant or not. For example: an uplift of six percent, with a ninety-five percent interval from plus one to plus eleven percent. That tells people both the likely size and the uncertainty. If the interval includes zero, you can't rule out no effect. And remember practical significance. With huge traffic, tiny effects become significant but may not be worth the maintenance. Decide in advance what effect is worth acting on. That's your MDE.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Launching without a sample size calculation. Picking an MDE smaller than any realistic effect, creating impossibly long tests. Reading p equals zero point zero five as a ninety-five percent chance that B is better. Ignoring confidence intervals. Stopping on a calendar date regardless of sample. And using conversion rate sample sizes for revenue metrics.
```

Scene 13 — Recap

```text
Recap. Chance makes identical pages differ, so plan tests with four inputs: baseline, minimum detectable effect, significance and power. Halving the effect quadruples the sample, and low baselines need more traffic. Convert sample size into whole weeks, and rethink anything longer than six to eight weeks. Report effects with confidence intervals and decide in advance what's worth acting on. Try this now: copy the calculator from the lesson text, plug in your own page's baseline and traffic, and write down the smallest effect you could realistically detect in four weeks.
```

</details>
